This famous Chinese dictionary 
contains 46,128 character entries,   and they are not in alphabetical order.

what's now routine was once innovative,   here's a concise history of how these dictionaries

you to look up one character you don't know. Our story starts in the shadow of a legend of once 
upon a time when Cāngjié invented the characters,   one version goes inspired by animal tracks. 
But whatever his intentions, already in the days   of Old Chinese, the classics were aging and 
the number and variety of characters daunted   readers. Two kinds of books began to be written 
to curb the confusion. The earliest dictionaries. Early dictionaries weren't dictionaries at all, 
not meant to be references for lovers of words   sitting on shelves. Instead, this first one is 
a "teaching aid" for interpreting the words in   the classics. It's called Ěryǎ (爾雅), The Ready 
Guide, and it classifies words into topics across   nineteen chapters based on their meanings. Without 
already knowing what our character means, we can't   look it up this way. So instead, let's browse the 
chapter names like explaining music, explaining   mountains or, this one's funny, it almost means 
explaining explaining. A simple example entry   from that chapter tells us that in this phrase 
(勿念，勿忘也) not lose means not forget. Note the   structure here: A means B, or actually A B yě. 
Here's another example that caught my eye（餥、餱，食也). I don't know this word. Or character (餥). In 
Classical Chinese, largely one word is one   character. I'll jot this one down. The entry says 
it means eating, which actually gives me a hint   about both the pronunciation and the meaning of 
a character I don't know. But back to that later. The most common Old Chinese character guides were 
training manuals for schoolchildren. The earliest,   Historian Zhòu's Primer (史籀篇), has since 
been lost. However, fragments of a work   in its footsteps have been unearthed. It's based 
around short lines organized into chapters where   each chapter rhymes, with a single rhyme 
every other line. This turns "recit[ing]   and memoriz[ing]" characters into something 
playful for students. This guide also takes the   step of grouping characters with similar visual

theories of language from the inside? Last time we filled a map with pins,   one pin for every example of something

do it. We ended with the map turned south-up   to represent people behind every pin. Let's

are aware of. How do we regard our own languages? That's not our first non-north-up map around 
here. Sometimes it takes turning a map   around to make sense of language. Since 
we're flipping linguistic perspectives,   it feels consistent to keep rotating

real stuff. Examples, examples! Possessive constructions got three mentions last

languages. In Jawoyn in northern Australia   it's "culturally anchored", not conceptualized as 
something that leashes the language to a person,   more like it links person and language

Can animals grammar? If there's any 
grammatical communication to be found   in species other than humans, surely it's in 
the complex songs of birds and whales. Today,   let's finish up my grammanimals journal 
with highlights from everything I read   about the animal linguistics 
of songbirds and cetaceans. Cetaceans are famous for the way 
they transmit high-range sounds   widely through their aquatic medium, including 
percussives, clicks, pulses, whistles and songs. Individual dolphins, as we learned early on in 
grammanimals, use unique signature whistles for   themselves when they get stranded apart from a 
group. They're also known for their high clicks,   for echolocation and maybe for more. Indeed, 
bottlenose dolphin clicks pass computational   tests for communication. Still, as ever, their 
function remains unclear. What is known enables   humans to create dolphin chat systems, allowing 
researchers to label objects with whistles,   or even let dolphins greet you with 
a whistle for your dolphin name. Orcas produce three types of vocalizations: 
short pulses for echolocation, tonal whistles   in close interactions, and a variety of 
pulsed calls. They're so "family oriented"   their burst pulses convey a signature for the 
whole pod not the individual. When confined   together they'll even develop a shared "'tank' 
call". Wondering what's here besides identity,   researchers processed tens of thousands 
of hours to find semantics and syntax   in "Orchive". (Heh, punny blend.) Using that 
method a new call type has already been found,   these "rare" ones from nonresident whales 
passing through that were previously undescribed. Then there are the singing whales. Male 
humpbacks repeat these extraordinary   songs composed of "units" nested inside of 
a "phrase"; phrases repeat within a "theme";   four to seven themes make a "song". With 
much study they've been heard to sing for   minutes up to even hours. Songs aren't fixed. 
Whales may alter songs gradually over time,   or in cases shift to a new song. But, let's 
say it together, the function remains unknown.   The compositionality paper I mentioned before 
wonders if it's an interesting case of wholes that   mean but parts that don't, while another paper is 
intrigued by the possibility of their ingressive   and egressive channels compositionally sending 
two messages at once. (Speculative; interesting.)   Still another follows the singing metaphor to 
its core: is this more music than language? Toothed whales don't sing, and yet there's 
still some love for them in the literature.   Narwhals are only recently heard 
producing two types of patterns,   and one of them appears in ten 
different types of sequences. Again,   hard to pinpoint the function, but these 
aren't individual signatures and they're not   about feeding. Toothed whale communication in general 
is still understudied compared to whalesong. Singers swim; they also fly. As is so well known, 
songbirds emit their sounds both beautiful and   harsh for territory or courtship. They take turns

still sensitive to reversing syllables   but not as much to rearranging the sequence. 
Budgies on the other hand have complex calls   and warble songs and readily detect 
song elements that don't belong. Order also matters to song sparrows, who sing 
about nine very unique songs built from a handful   of elements. In what's been called a "'Dear 
Enemy' relationship", they start territory   negotiations from shared song conventions in 
a "graded" call system that works like this.   First, commit another bird's singing repertoire to 
memory. Then, start singing. When another bird   responds, you have four options: continue the 
same song, switch to a different shared song,   switch to an unshared song, or stop singing. 
One researcher says this is all just a conflict   management system, where the whole thing 
means how much you're willing to de-escalate. The common name for you just 
got me the wrong search results,   so ~ "四十雀" / "Parus minor" ~ combines an 
alert "chicka" with a recruitement call to   form a new "alert-recruitment" 
complex for mobbing a threat.   Papers are willing to admit the birds do form 
and interpret this as a compositional call,   perhaps one of the clearest examples we've met, 
which would be amazing, but questions remain. Chickadees, ah! They sing four notes in order. 
They can sing one note multiple times, step up to   the next note, or skip notes, but they don't for 
example start a sequence on the fourth note and   then sing the first. Individual notes may have 
specific meanings or functions. Some sequences   might, too: this disturbance sequence seems built 
from a restless move note and a redirect note. And then there are corvids. They gesture and make 
a range of calls. American Crows more than 30,   Northern Ravens over 70, Australian corvids more 
than 80. Raven vocalization varies by continent,   location, even mated pair. Much of 
the attention goes toward not how they   call but how they think, corvid smarts. Testing 
several ravens on trials designed for primates,   the ravens proved a match for apes in 
most cognitive tasks. One researcher asks,   "Are corvids 'feathered apes'?" Watch how they 
stack up when it comes to recursion. (Ask a   syntactician or programmer, or apparently 
a crow!) Take a look at these brackets.   How would you arrange them? Train humans, macaques 
and crows to nest one pair inside the other,   and the crows outperform macaques and about 
as well as human children. Add new brackets   and an extra layer of nesting, and the crows 
still do exceptionally well at three levels   of recursion. So this paper concludes that "crows 
join humans" in our ability to think recursively. "Bird brains" are capable brains. But now 
we've gone from animal signals to animal   cognition. I doubt at heart our speculative   curiosity was rooted in a desire to 
study signal sequence structure. When we find out that corvids nest brackets,

windows confronts us with an inconsistency:   how much do we really consider them 
and their communication systems? Like,   smack in the middle of studying about orcas

A pictorial manuscript, its pages covered in 
paintings. But what if I told you you're reading   written words? This script from Mexico defies our 
concept of what a writing system should be.  So, how does it work? Whose words are they? 
How do you read them? Why did it remain   relevant even after colonization？Here's a 
story about how pictography tells stories. Today, meet a script unlike other scripts 
I've studied – and I worked through this   "Aztec" handbook! If I'm to hand you the 
loose end of the thread I've been pulling   to understand the tale of these iconic texts, 
I have to ask a seemingly unrelated question:   what do you think of strawberries? In strawberry 
fields of California, workers speak a language   you might never've heard before, or even heard of. 
In fact they more than other agricultural workers   are likely to have this language barrier: not 
speaking Spanish. To overcome obstacles, look at   these glyphs – a community group organizes events 
and helpful radio messages under the banner of   imagery that ties back to pages painted by their 
ancestors in their home landscape – mountains,   valleys, coasts where people speak rich language 
varieties given the umbrella label Mixtec. A map of their lands in what's now southern Mexico 
caught the eye of archaeologist Alfonso Caso in   the 1940s. This old colonial map depicts part 
of La Mixteca at the turn of the year 1576. In an eye-catching mix of Indigenous and European 
styles, iconic glyphs dot and criss-cross and   surround the map, centered on the town of Chiyo Cahnu,  and then grow out and drape 
down one side. The people seated facing each  other – these figures look familiar. In fact, they 
appear in a whole group of pictorial manuscripts   dating to the Postclassic, around 900 to 1500. They're 
called pictorials because, look at them – scene   after scene alive with characters, places, symbols. Caso wonders, could that map be   a sort of Rosetta Stone? A key to unlocking 
the names in this much earlier collection? He's onto something. Glyphs follow a pattern 
of naming people with two names. First,   numbers of disks next to day signs are 
dates. Newborns would get a calendar name to   connect them to time through their birthdate. 
There's another sign for their given name,   like Spiderweb of Rain. The two names are 
called dzevui /sɨ́w(ɨ́)/ and dzeque /sɨkɨ/. Dzevui, dzeque – we can already hear how from 
here on out language skills become critical,   specifically the skills from those farm 
fields. So a word about the language. I   don't speak it; I've been relying on 
the guidance of Gabina Pérez Jiménez,   who writes about her modern variety and the old 
Postclassic language. One fact for starters:   hers is a tonal language in 
a family of tonal languages. Like names throughout Mexico, "Mixtec" 
comes from "Aztec", Nāhuatl: mixtēcah,   basically cloud-people. ("Aztec" is definitely 
not "Mixtec", though they will cross paths in   this story.) Mi[ʃ]teca to mi[s]teca even mi[ks]teca...  Mixtecos themselves in their diverse 
dialects combine two words: People or Community   and Rain or Raingod, with bountiful ways of 
saying "rain". Ñuù Dzàui or Sàvi, the Rain Nation. Language at hand and in mind, you can now identify 
individual iyàdzehe and iyà, the ladies and lords,   the ñuù, the towns they ruled, and 
follow them across the pages. The basic glyphs in their names stand not for a 
sound or syllable but a whole word. (Welcome back,   logograms, to my channel!) Some examples: a flower 
is a common element in names, and it represents   ità, the word for flower. (Or tonally ita in 
another variety.) Likewise, a mountain is yucu,   found at the start of some important place names. 
The motif shown off here is ñuu, the word we just   learned for people or town or commu~ w-we'll 
return to its weighty historical significance. Logograms put together are read together. Simplest 
case to me is one word beside another. So Lady 10   Flower, that's her calendar name, has a 
given name with two words: Spiderweb of   the Rain. She's seated on a town sign, ñuu, 
with flints atop: Ñuù Yuchì, Town of Flints. Glyphs can join, conflate. The Raingod 
from another angle, Dzaui again,   wears the town motif we learned for ñuu: 
Ñuu Dzaui, People of the Rain. This is year,   cuiyà, famous among the in-crowd as 
the A-O sign. Ok, fair term for it.   But then one of four year signs gets tied up 
in it: the Year 6 Flint, the Year 13 Reed. Even choice of pigment can be meaningful, 
very much like I showed in Nāhuatl writing   in a bonus video for patrons. The left 
column of that map contains a place name   with the ñuu motif, but it's painted 
black for the color word. Sure enough,   there's a corresponding town 
known today as Ñuù Túún. Not every reading is so literal, funny word 
given this script. Earlier we acknowledged the   language is tonal. How tonal? Try: earth ñuhu, 
fire ñuhù, deity ñùhù; ñùhù–ñuhù–ñuhu. Scribes   indulge in this and play with tonal puns. Here 
are two hometowns. Chiyo Cáhnu, Big Altar. (Hey, that's the one from the map!)   Cáhnu means "big". The Aztec route 
would've been to enlarge the glyph,   but here a human bends and breaks a 
piece of the town sign. Bend/break   cahnù is a pun for big cáhnu. Next 
place has eyes, nuù, and crossed bars,   crossed being ndìjin. But tonal punning flips 
ndìjin to ndijìn: it's Ndijìn Nuù, Good View. The script also abounds in idioms, sayings known 
in Spanish as difrasismos, two-phrases-isms,   kindred in heart to any lover of a good kenning. 
Writers blend these parallelisms like puzzles   into the scene. The mat and throne, yuvui tayù, 
together beneath married rulers mean rulership.   Protagonists throwing darts into a place name? 
Nduvua iyà, "arrows of the lords", conquest.   One more: tnuhu/tùhun yaà, "the word the song", 
is flowery poetry for lineage history. (And   if it's starting to sound like the 
same old story of rulers and ruled,   well, hold your assumptions. We will cycle 
back when we ponder the word ñuu.) This degree of puzzling out and give-and-take 
is expected from old Mexico. But in one way   Mixtec writing stands out: not a single phonetic 
sign in sight! Even the Aztec Empire's script   writes phonetic syllables. This though,   from the way writing systems experts define 
writing, is pure logography, word writing. Beyond individual signs, words also carry memories of composition. 
Scribes are those who write and paint the   words and songs, meaning document the history. Red lines   yuq yuq "follow the furrow" to guide 
our eyes around the screenfold pages. Now you could follow centuries of dates, names,   puns and parallelisms around 
the pages in a yuq yuq pattern. Or you could try to follow. Because these disparate 
deciphered pieces don't connect into a unified   linguistic text. Could the entire page ever be read? Or

Theirs are the definitions I'd meet as I sat   gangly-legs-folded-awkwardly on my bag, tucked

us we're in the presence of writing and not solely   continuous narrative art. (Am I the only one at

words, puns and puzzles? Feels familiar.) "Interpretation" here doesn't mean it's up to

alphabetic parallels. Two more named people   sit in marriage, so 6 Death married 2 Flower. Then

the alphabet: 6 Death Rain-Sun married 2 Flower   Jewel-Quetzal who came from Chiyo Yuhu. It's 
the same sentence side by side in two scripts. In short, readings are rooted in Indigenous 
usage, colonial texts and living voices,   and books must be both "deciphered" and 
"interpreted" in order to be "properly"   read. Such a perspective shift demands a new 
definition. So gather the old ones, cross out   proto vs true, bring back the people. Writing 
is simply "markings which represent language". Now, I don't know if I endorse this, I just can't

we need to call pictorials writing to say why   they matter – they could actually represent 
an advancement over phonetic scripts, not a   step backwards. Advancement? Yes, partly because 
Mixtec manuscripts do something multilingually   clever we'll meet soon, partly because of what

writing-to-interpretation cycle worked,   and who played what roles at each stage. Some mistakes are just typos. This painter 
missed a disk, writing 12 Serpent when   it's thirteen. Ok. Or this yuq yuq starts 
up top with Lady 6 Flower. However,   her calendar name is really 6 Reed 
as in this codex, not 6 Flower. Other mistakes are more than an oops! This manuscript has 
two sides. The guidebook I've been using notes the   front is "polished and flawless", but the back is 
"sloppy", full of the kinds of mistakes a painter   would make who knew neither the local history nor 
the local style, but copied another manuscript. Alongside evidence of copying, pictorials 
depict singing and orchestras playing,   and alphabetics record terms for 
lineage songs. It sets the stage for   a whole interpretive cycle that blurs 
distinctions between written and oral   societies. Instead of my Writing Bee 
where writers write marks read by readers,   here's a Ñuu Savi model. Wordsmiths or singers 
use flowery speech that's painted in a pictorial   source. Scribes copy that source without 
necessarily knowing the story or the lyrics.   Trained interpreters perform as bards, reading 
or singing the words aloud amidst an audience. And why does it matter that people were reading 
and writing history this way? Well,   consider an intriguing advantage of their script. Writing definers routinely highlight 
pictography's disadvantages.  (That's how I first heard about it – all its "problems".) Yet in one respect this script shines. At the outset I animated a page unfurling and 
trusted we shared a gut instinct this doesn't   look like writing. Over there, walls of text. Sure. 
Can't tell what's going on unless you know the   scripts. Meanwhile, here you had a shot 
at guessing it's about two people, maybe even   two people getting married even before watching. (Incidentally, 
those walls of text? Also about marriages!) Well how to leverage this legibility at a glance in

readable in more than one language at once.   Take the Raingod glyph – it could unify

A-O you've come to know and love for  cuiya, "the year". But a leaf grows out of it,

glyph: they diverge on their words for year and   converge on the date. And the alphabetic text 
confirms it's bilingually Aztec and Mixtec. This unique manuscript is a book of accounts

village-state. The pictorials we've   met so far tell of how ñuu rise amidst 
a crisis at the close of the Classic   Period in a misty foundation honored via the 
Sacred Bundle. Throughout the Postclassic,   ñuu form elaborate ties through relationships and 
conflict. Rather than control land and resources though,   the mat the throne, rulers, cultivate 
ties and reach out for items needed to   maintain those ties. Power over feathers not 
over feed. How does one keep such ties   for so long? Document them in pictorial books; 
periodically publicly perform pictorial books. The ñuu now subjugated, its nature

Europeanly and colonizer messages natively. Nobles navigate power by pictorializing their

exhibits, become universals not particulars,   for everyone just not for them. Ñuu comes 
to connote erosion and endurance. As one   of those others mentioned here, I'll take this

that here to our spelling's phonemic to even, we have our own orthography games, thank 
you! Ah, and that's a whole other animation. Now, to the newcomers out there, and as a

Each of you takes a turn. You get a word. The word's pronounced. You have to repeat it, spell it 
out letter by letter, then repeat it again. Spell it wrong, you're knocked out. Do that in rounds of 
harder and harder words until one speller remains. (From asking around, some adults to this day 
can't let go of the word that got them out.) My dream of a nightmare writing bee wraps this 
kernel of a concept within a larger spectacle. It alters its inspiration in at least two basic

one language but every known script-language pair across the globe and across history. You randomly

the original bee has a historical arc that doubles down on marks-on-the-page definitions of writing: 
from spelling for reading's sake to spelling for spelling's sake to spelling for winning's sake.

even two beautiful creators out there already have something to say about that.) My writing bee puts

starts narrowly focused on the symbols, limiting ourselves to scrutinizing marks on the page, or the wheel. These are the familiar boundaries 
of academic definitions of writing. But then this game zooms out to include the roles 
of a writer writing and a reader reading, presumably also implying some surrounding studies 
of society and writing. For both roles, it tests what makes a script harder or easier using a 
simple mapping between symbols and phonemes. This function maps phonemes to glyphs and 
glyphs to phonemes as a way to determine a script's consistency for writers and readers. 
Many glyphs mapping to one linguistic unit is hard on the writer. One glyph mapping 
to many linguistic units is hard on the reader. One-to-one mapping is easy on 
both roles. Many to many is hard on both. So when I put you in the hotseat and test you

inconsistencies that make a script challenging. So that's how I think this wheel worked. Now 
how it doesn't. Here, take these examples I've been handwriting. From my rough calligraphy 
practice, these are ones that give me pause when, late at night, the writing bee pops 
into my mind. I'll share the slower, messier thinking-while-inking with patrons. 
Here I'll summarize so we can keep the pen flowing. (Please don't be impressed; I dabble to 
a fault. Wiser people have devoted their hands to each script, and they've 
inspired me. Links to them below.) In our worldwide writing bee, what kinds of 
roles have writers and readers played? Well, they've negotiated and adjusted a familiar 
system that covers an enormous diversity of languages and even contradictory conventions. One 
20th-century writer innovated this handcrafted alphabet to represent a language family 
and its emerging koiné. For millennia readers and writers have maintained this old 
script through diacritics and sound mergers, going on to inspire others who also contributed

cropped passive suffix in one word block, and then in the next block write

within our understanding of human grammar systems,   so as to be genuinely impressed 
by their linguistic complexity if   we should ever spot parallels? Dial 
down. Start small, with calls that   sound more like expressive syllables than 
content-full linguistic words or sentences. The first animal language chapter I ever read 
quotes Alice in _Through the Looking Glass_,   who likens meaningless communication to kittens 
saying purr no matter what. Yet real felines,   cats, felids broadly defined, make a dozen calls. 
Four Panthera species share at least a hiss,   prusten, roar, growl and puff. Cheetahs let 
out several calls, from an agressive hiss   to a unique bird-like chirp. Most common are 
meows, which get directed toward conspecifics,   members of the same species, other 
cheetahs. Meows function to maintain   contact or beckon. In captivity, they extend 
meows to another species, their human keepers,   to request help or food. Cheetah and felid 
vocalization generally is a young field,   such solitary species, but seems like their 
calls are limited in number and there from birth. The three living species of elephants produce 
several calls. Notice the word "calls" again. It's   the term I find throughout the literature for these types 
of vocalizations, single acoustic animal signals   we can put a name to. Calls often have variants, across or within species. 
Elephant calls include trumpets and impressive   infrasonic growls and rumbles. There's quite 
a lot of difference in the vocalizations of   Elephas maximus versus the two Loxodonta species, 
like many ways to turn a single non-aggressive   "let's go" into call-response exchanges to signal 
"get moving" or even into a grumbly bee alarm.   So when an elephant's experiencing discomfort 
or heightened emotions, in Tanzania expect   to hear trumpets where in Sri Lanka you'd 
hear a roar or the species-unique longroar. Giraffe communication is poorly studied, with 
just 21 papers in the 60 years between 1958   and 2018. They were once declared 
unable to vocalize at all. But a   handful of studies caught them making rather 
elusive noises, including humming in zoos   at night. Now we can talk about four types 
of known giraffe sounds: hisses, snorts,   low-hums and infrasonic signals. Their functions 
remain unknown, at least as of the paper I read   from the end of the 2010s that asks the wonderful 
question, "How do giraffes locate one another?" Giant otters, who live and fish and 
cooperate and groom in the Amazon   rainforests, make multiple calls. Adults: 
22 calls plus a rare mating call. Cubs:   11 calls. Their group calls signal social 
coordination and location. They have sounds   for playing or soothing or keeping visual 
contact or coordinating hunts. Alarm calls,   including snorts to warn of caimans. And wavering 
screams when juveniles get lost. They also make   calls that signal their own identity, voicing 
their own individual otter signatures.   (It won't be the last time we meet such calls.) And then there are the begging bouts. When a conspecific 
catches a fish you want, you can start off a   begging bout with a begging call. If that's not 
enough, intensify your begging with a begging   scream. The fish-having otter then growls to 
defend their catch against begging callers. We talked felid repertoires, what about 
canids? I read about the 20-plus calls   voiced by painted dogs, which they make in 
single "syllables", "bursts" of syllables,   and "bouts" of two or more bursts, including 
in long-distance exchanges called "volleys".   Among canids their barks are particularly 
varied, with six bark subclasses recorded.   Our friendly dog-dogs have a range of calls 
including group cohesion howls, play huffs,   warning or guarding growls, and of course the 
short-range bark. (Which you might hear in the background!) They vocalize more than wolves,   and vary their barks for situations ranging 
from greeting to warning to playing. It's   notoriously catchy, here's a fancy word for you: 
allelomimetic, meaning conspecifics likely start   barking too. Those conspecifics can tell a dog's 
emotional state and size from calls, and amazingly   enough so can fellow humans. That's right, you can 
guess a lot about a dog just from hearing a call. Signals are not just acoustic though. Making noise 
isn't the only way to go. Like humans,   nonhuman animals communicate with other 
modalities. Meet the jacky dragon from   southeastern Australia. These coastal lizards 
use several visual movements to communicate,   including tail flicks and push-ups. If 
you want to be able to read a typical   jacky dragon action sequence, learn the 
following order exactly: tail-flick,   backward-forward-arm-wave, push-up-body-rock. 
Interestingly, the order does matter. This   sequence does not elicit the same response from 
conspecifics when reversed in playback. Ah,   but I'm stumbling ahead of us. We'll come back 
to the potential for meaningful sequences. Many of us say that animals are "talking to 
us" when they do stuff like this, but how do   we know these repertoires so far are signals and 
not just displays? I'm avoiding dragging us into   the theory side for hours, but let's take a couple 
steps into the animal communication research and   demarcate displays, signals, cues. Here is a list, 
Morton's list of motivation-structural rules.  High/low/raspy/tonal, what if these aren't 
word-like cues, merely emotions on display,   in sound and visual form? If heightened signals 
are merely direct shows of heightened emotions,   then call them syllables, maybe, but 
they're not really human-like words. Does this otter mean give me fish or let the 
begging ritual commence or just I'm expressing   emotions? Well, consider rats. They do 
amazing ultrasonic vocalizations, USVs,   associated with social roles, territory 
and courtship. Rats have two ranges,   a small range around a lower frequency and 
a large range around a higher one. Instead   of 22 kHz and 50 kHz I'll call them "low" and 
"high" but keep in mind they're both ultrasonic,   high pitch to inaudible to us. Lows are associated with 
freezing behavior and negative emotions, and   highs with approaching and positive emotions. Simple enough. Then again they seem  to chain high-highs or low-highs in sequences   and have even been heard letting out a low 
together with a high trill in one single-breath   call. The meanings though are unclear. Their squeaky 
syllables and sequences remain undeciphered. So I suspect at this point, in our videos-long 
animated search for animal grammar, we're at the point where we need to think more about meaningful words. Semantics. 
After all, last time we saw how a key component   of human grammar is putting together meaningful 
pieces. So let's wonder how meaningful these   animal call and gesture pieces, these 
"syllables", actually are. Next time. Thank you for watching and thanks to patrons 
for supporting my Grammaninals. We're halfway   through the series now. Lower numbers of views 
than in the past but I'll repeat this is joyful   work for me. Let's see it through to the 
end, and stay tuned for other linguistic   projects I have in the works. Stick around 
and subscribe for animals and languages.

by the fire during a rainstorm on a piece of paper... wait for it!... erased! This is a garden path sentence, meant
to lull you into one interpretation before yanking you back at the end with that verb. (It's funny because I could've said "wrote"
but you had to stay tuned for that "erased"!) And yet this order – the writer a word erased,
the capybara food ate, the baker a cake baked – this is the most popular order!? Why? To understand why SOV is so widespread and
actually less unpredictable than my silly drawn-out example suggests, we need to rethink
what we're really after when we look to spot a sentence's esses, oes and vees. Linguists out there might be eminently concerned
with the placement of objects and subjects relative to verbs. What about you, how do you go through life? When I picture an event and I try to communicate
it, I feel less invested in arcane questions of syntax. It's more about:  who? did? what? What happens if we treat this that way, like
more than a sentence? It's an event, a story about what people and
animals do in the world: someone doing something. The baker baked a cake. Or to put it in SOV, the baker a cake baked. To match this word order's fame, linguists
gifted me with an analogy so fitting that it inspired the artwork for this video. Lest you think this was all just set dressing. Within this event there's an action and the
roles played in performing that action. These roles of performer and performed-on
are the actor/agent and the undergoer/patient. (Patient always sounds like such a strong word.
I realize it means sufferer. I don't want you to suffer from my verbs!
 Memorable tho.) So now we have an action, a patient and an agent. How are we supposed to order them? Well, do we need to? We're two videos deep into this word order
stuff and haven't even asked ourselves if it's necessary in the first place. If we know there's an action, actor and acted-upon,
couldn't we get by on meaning alone? If roles provide enough of a clue, it's a
strong cue... why order at all? Here, daydream along with me once more. I already asked you to imagine yourself speaking
an SOV language. Now stretch a bit and wonder: what if we had
free word order? Ate food the capybara. A nail hammered the worker. Words the writer scribbled. None of these was in English-friendly SVO. Were they really that hard to follow? So many of our sentences are like this – you
can reverse the words, but it's harder to reverse the meaning. We bring our everyday expectations to sentences:
food isn't the one doing the eating, nails aren't hammering, words aren't
scribbling people. Indeed, many languages do stop there, staying
flexible, leveraging context. And if your language were among those ever-flexers,
you would join this no-dominant-order crowd. They make up a detectable percentage of languages
alongside the six types. They're the ones with the ant as their mascot! All languages flex, but their whole thing
is harnessing enough underlying flexibility that it's hard to pin them down to one neutral
word order. Join in on their flexible antics and try to
follow what I'm about to say: baked a cake the baker, the baker a cake baked, a cake the baker baked. Do you picture anything but a cake being baked,
and a baker doing the baking? Then again, try this one, imagine it flexibly
ordered: the cat the raven watches. Who's watching whom? Gotcha! That was my attempt at a reversible sentence. Since you can think about it both ways, through
either perspective, the cat or the raven, at least out of context, what hints or clues could we use to keep track
of the roles of agent and patient? Well, tag them to track them, mark the participants! The academic literature uses the term "the
entities", sounds ominous to me, but ok: mark the entities involved in the action, with
case markers on your nouns. Or mark the action, let the verb tag who's
involved. They're useful not just for obvious cases,
haha. These basic, neutral sentences aren't always
common sentences in a language. In practice, many languages find them awkward,
unwieldy, a bit too deliberate. Instead, why not drop, when already understood,
the agent, or the patient, or both. Cake baked. You know who? Baker baked. You know what? Or even just: baked. Must I repeat roles to remind you of what
we both already know? Now when it comes to "watched the cat" versus
"the cat watched", in SOV those could otherwise look identical, so case markers help us distinguish
them. Flexible languages use both of those cues,
meaning and marking. SOV languages do, too. Even if they keep verbs final like in our
garden path sentence, they might scramble all the ~ ahem ~ entities before the verb. It's no problem keeping track of them if you
mark cases! And woah do SOV languages love their subject
and object markers. That's great and all but it doesn't get us
to the heart of SOV languages. The cue, THE cue that defines them is the
one that brings us here today: word order. Pick one, rigidify one, grammaticalize one
like most languages do, and we end up back at this popularity contest. So why did the SOV sentence get the starring
role in this video about word order? Its agent told it to be patient before they
say "action"! Cringe linguistic jokes aside, the sentence
we're after really has an agent-like subject and a patient-like object. So an SOV sentence reflects an event order
of agent, then patient, then action. SOV's – or APV's – starring role in the
word order story has attracted streams of researched attention devoted to uncovering
how SOV earned its reputation. Among its standout superlatives, this one
struck me as the superlativest. Why is Agent-Patient-Action called, in grandiose
terms, the universal order, the word order of the language of thought? Alright, before I give you the answer, try
to give me one first. If a linguist took your own language off the
table and asked you to gesture or arrange pictures or invent your own way to communicate
an event, what order would you use? Events. Most events we've been talking about have
a very human basic shape: someone acted on something. Baker cake baked, capybara corn ate, writer
word wrote. These events are non-reversible – even if
we swap orders, we can discern what they're about. A basic someone-did-something event in our
world has a human acting on an inanimate object. In other words, many of our most basic sentences
map to events where we're thinking about a who acting on a what. Like the baker baking a cake. Or yes, the
capybara eating corn. Ok. An experiment tasks you with showing me three
pictures of a human, acting, on something inanimate. How would you arrange them? Answers will vary, but an easy guess is, surprisingly
regardless of our language, we tend to prefer humans before inanimate objects before actions. Seemingly implying SOV. Sentences subject humans to special treatment,
and they object to inanimates. (Not done with the puns yet; I'll back off
but allow me a few more before credits roll.) So you're a budding language, and you're tasked
with canonizing one order. You notice that the humans really like the-person
some-thing did. You do some linguistics: turn that into roles,
set up the who that did it, the what that was done, then the doing, hit play, call action! Voilà, it's Agent-Patient-Verb. Make it canon in your language's grammar,
and you've got Subject-Object-Verb. If this is how things work, then SOV is the
syntactic layer of an underlying human-inanimate-action preference. Short and sweet, SOV is the word order of
your thoughts. If true, when it comes to events with animate
doers acting on inanimate undergoers, we basically think in SOV! Unless we switch things up. Say both the agent and the patient are animate,
and both feel like possible agents. Well, that was the cat and raven predicament. What happens then? Save that for someday when we get to SVO. So is that it? Are SOV languages SOV because of this underlying
universal preference? We all think in SOV, everyone else is being
derivative? Not quite. When it comes to individual languages and
their word order preferences, there's something far more influential than the universal language-of-thought
factor. Last time for understanding OSV, I drew a
model. Bring it back in. If OSV's rarity taught us how all languages
flex, here's what we can learn from SOV's popularity. I've spent most of our minutes focused on
one influencer, that universal factor. There are other influences though, namely
other languages: genealogically your language's family, and areally your language's neighbors. Then there's flexibility, an underlying spectrum
of word order variation. All these influences get funneled through
filters that constrict your sentences or bend them to your situation and surroundings. I'm trying to make sure SOV is watchable even
if you haven't seen OSV, but this model reminds me, wow did we cover a lot last time! From everything so far, I've led us to expect
that SOV is SOV because of that universal factor. It's the word order of thought, after all. But calculate out these factors, and this
might all be less about universality, more about family. Universals do influence and predict a language's
word order. So do the word orders in neighboring languages,
if less so. By far though the biggest factor is the word
order in the language your language descends from. If OSV compelled us to add flexibility as
an input into our model, SOV teaches us we should split the "other languages" input into
two, since areas and families have very different influences on a language's word order. It doesn't matter how hard you think, or who
you meet, your words can't escape being sentenced to your family's influence. And in fact that bias is so strong that
if we remove family bias, the rankings look very different. Strip out family bias, and while we're at
it, areal bias too. (Areal in linguistics is about areas, places
and neighbors, not skies.) Who emerges on top? Who gets reranked? Well, factoring out genealogical and areal
advantages, most orders shrink in popularity or stay flat... except flex and SOV. And that gap between first place SOV and
second place SVO opens to a chasm. It seems merely a few families, and not independent
facts about languages, are responsible for SVO being as much of a runner up as it is. That's a good place to admit this commenter
is correct, there's much more worth saying about the role of colonialism in the spread
of SVO families, coming mostly at the expense of SOV languages. Empires and colonization pushing SVO languages
ahead, that's full of stories. Power and its impacts on languages strike again... So, is SOV because was SOV because had been
SOV therefore will be SOV? Until forced to become SVO? Well of course history isn't all force and
family, and language change isn't simply  SVO vs SOV. One fantastic exception is an Oceanic language
that recently in history made an unusual shift from the most popular order to the rarest. Yes, Tobati went from SOV to OSV. That's the exception. It's still an SOV world out there. Tantalizingly, is there another piece to this
story? What if SOV-in-the-brain-ism is great for
thought but not for spreading information? What if all languages are shifting from an
SOV past to a future where we'll be living in an SVO world? But that's for another time. Until then, humans keep being agents of patient
verbiage, continuing to object before we act. SOV, you remain number one, and are if anything
undercounted. Thank you for spending another video with me
getting animated about word orders, first rare and now popular. Consider joining the hundreds of patrons behind
the scenes of my linguistic tales, including the names scrolling here, that's one of my
patronage rewards. Most importantly, stick around and subscribe
for more about our world full of beautiful languages and cultures.

ending because they are not in any of the other   forms. It's a sign with no expression, no sound 
but still a meaning, and he calls it a zero sign. Crucially, this is still purely the result 
of happenstance. Summarizing but with much commentary from me, these may have and did come from 
these nouns changing vertically through time,   but if we switch and look at them horizontally,   only compared to other Czech noun forms, we get 
this nice contrast of meaningful grammatical   somethings versus one meaningful grammatical 
nothing. The thing about this, for Saussure,   is that over time particular changes affect 
this or that point, but never the entire block of   the system of language as a whole. The history 
that gave Czech this zero doesn't define how   it works. Its use in the noun system does. I 
guess that's fortunate for students because it   means you don't need to know the changes leading 
up to Czech just to learn the language today. Hold this zero firmly in one hand and 
with the other turn back the pages to   signifier and signified. We studied for this 
moment. The basics are about to dawn on us.  So, what is a Czech genitive plural ending that 
doesn't have any sound? Well, what is a sign that   still represents some mental concept, signified, 
but has no sensed expression, signifier?   Ah, a zero sign! And from there 
this whole debacle spirals out. But for today let's take a 
breath and pack our backpacks.   Since you're still here, and assuming 
Saussure made sense, we're in a better   position to give that animation one 
more try, or better yet that podcast. After your feedback, I'm glad we took a moment 
before closing the book on zeros. I'm eager to   make progress on two aspiring future tales 
and tinker with my animation toolkit to   prepare it for whichever's next. In the 
meantime, please support those whose   languages and cultures anchor the content of this 
channel within an ocean of – ah, still too poetic,   be direct: visit the links, press the 
buttons, I'll catch you next time.

## 22. What makes Papuan languages so unique? - features from the area with the most languages
**Channel:** NativLang | **Views:** 188K | **Date:** 3 years ago | **Duration:** 12:44 | **ID:** fkhRuoJmSao

### Transcript:
Melanesia floats upon a bed of eleven seas,
one large island north o f Australia flanked by many more islands both east and west. This
region is home to not only more seas but far more languages than anywhere else on Earth,
total, per person and per area. Most of these diverse tongues are unlike the large families
they're surrounded by. They're called "Papuan". Why are they so unique? What makes Papuan
languages Papuan? First let's spend time talking language geography
to understand where Papuan people live and speak. Their language map once stretched well
into central Indonesia, until an infamous 1815 eruption wiped out the trading state
of Tambora, its people and their Papuan language. Today the west edge of the Papuasphere is
scattered across islands: Alor, Pantar, Timor, North Maluku, but not over to Sulawesi nor
up towards the Philippines. Then we reach the shores of the vast and mountainous Niugini,
where most but not close to all languages are Papuan. To the east lie archipelagos:
Bismarcks, Bougainville, Solomon Islands, down to the Reefs and Santa Cruz Islands between
Malaita, Makira and Vanuatu. Counting and classifying their languages continues
to be one of the ultimate linguistic challenges. I could fill a whole video with the debated
counts of Papuan languages and families, but some other time. Because behind all that, there's a really
simple story sketched in colonial and even recent academic writing on the area. Papuan
people migrate across these islands long, long ago and settle in as kind of the original
hunter-gatherers here. Much later comes this spread of Austronesian, an extensive language
family (that's an understatement) whose speakers sail across seas from Taiwan to Borneo, the Philippines, New Guinea,
as far as Aotearoa, Rapa Nui and Madagascar. Austronesians introduce technology and mark
a linguistic and cultural shift. Or so the story goes. Hence the term "Papuan"
becomes useful as a residual basket for the many diverse families and isolates that have this one thing in common:
they are languages in this ellipse that are not Austronesian and not Australian. Because of the sheer number of languages here,
the presumably long time depth, and how difficult they are to classify, it's hard not to wonder,
what makes Papuan languages unique? Well, let's study their features. Along the way
hold in the back of your mind that residual term and that simple story. One way to get a sense for Papuan languages
is to listen for their characteristic sound. Despite their vast diversity and apparent
unrelatedness to each other, there are some running acoustic themes. Very few consonants and five simple vowels.
The classic example is Rotokas, its sounds confidently represented by an alphabet of
just twelve letters. This leaves the region devoid of sounds found in many of the world's
languages: often missing /p/ or /g/, rarely distinguishing /l/ from /r/, sometimes having
neither, even lacking /ŋ/, very unlike their Austronesian neighbors who can easily start
words with "ng-"s. When it comes not to what's missing but what's
present, two more complex sorts of sounds stand out to me. Their fancy names are labiovelars
and prenasalized stops. The first are the two-in-one sounds you heard me articulate
when talking about West Africa, like ɡ͡b and ŋ͡m. The second combine nose and mouth
sounds into what is here often just the basic way to say your [mb]s, [nd]s, [ŋg]s. These
Papuan traits may have forever influenced the Oceanic branch of Austronesian from their
very earliest Proto Oceanic days. One Oceanic language even combined both these potential
Papuanisms into this one wonderful unit - yes, it's one single phoneme. Dare I try? /ᵑᵐg͡bʷa/,
ok somebody out there please do better! Papuan sign systems are also unique.
While reading more and more about Melanesia's spoken languages I was lucky to come across
research on New Guinea's nucleated sign networks, which are throwing wrenches into standard
understandings of "village", "shared" or "rural homesign". If we really want to show off Papuan features,
the standouts are less detectable if you don't know the languages. That is, unless we talk
grammar. I know, that means jargony terminology and examples from languages you do not speak.
But to capture your attention, imagine along with me: what if your language were Papuan,
how would it work? Start where most do: word order. Austronesian
verbs come first, before objects, often before subjects. But Papuan verbs are final. With
exceptions - there are Papuan SVO languages along Niugini's edges. So while Oceanic languages
are roughly like "cooked grandma yams", typical Papuan languages say "grandma yams cooked". When your language configures itself as a
"cooked yams" or a "yams cooked" type, other words also fall into predictable places.
Austronesian VO languages have prepositions, like "with yams", while on Niugini languages
overwhelmingly opt for postpositions: "yams with". Zoom in on the verb. Papuan verbs are rich
in affixes. Future tense suffixes, so instead of "will eat" it's more like "eat-'ll". Past
tense suffixes that distinguish remoteness, like "cooked today" versus "cooked yesterday"
versus "cooked ago". The verb itself marks who's involved in the action. In Niugini you
usually attach, not pronouns, but an object marker before and a subject marker after.
Instead of "you will cook them", with a lonely bare verb, try packing all of that info into "'you yams em-cook-'ll-ya". Papuan nouns and pronouns are simpler. No
plural nouns, so instead of "a yam" or "yams", it's "yam cooked" whether that means one or
many. Forget cases, even on pronouns, so "me" is always "me" not "I". Unlike nouns, Papuan
pronouns do have impressive numbers, including words for you,
two of you, three of you, or many of you. But pronouns may do two odd things
compared to Oceanic: no third persons, no "it", no "them", and no distinction between
inclusive us, you-me, and exclusive us, me and others but not you. That's such an Austronesian
thing but hardly a Papuan thing. When something's possessed... hah, not by spirits
though there are stories from this area, no, when one noun has another noun,
some languages say "grandma yam-her", others "grandma's yam". Two things to note: the possessor-possessed
word order and what's called "head marking", showing possession on what you have instead
of who has it. Let that sink in because possessive relationships get trickier. Papuan can distinguish
many possessive classes. At their simplest, their possessions are alienable - our house
is "us house" - or inalienable - these hands are "our hand". (No plurals, remember?) Notably,
Oceanic distinguishes these too, even like in Tahiti well away from the Papuashpere.
And it might all trace back to Papuan influence. Now that I got us tangled in a grammatical
rainforest, allow me to cut at a few vines that strike my fancy before we move towards
our last section today. First, don't worry about passive voice. It's not an option here.
Also, negation works in unusual ways for our world, Bantu languages notwithstanding. Negatives
follow verbs, "yam-that cook don't" for "don't cook that yam", and this may be a very old
Papuanism. Languages may do double or even triple negation, what is that like "cook no
not don't"!? Ok, zoom out to consider two last distinctions
involving stories and bodies. Storytellers set up events with a theme built around nouns,
then chain together verb-heavy events. You won't hear sentences like "we cooked three
tasty yams". No. Theme these with "so, yams three", then distribute the rest into "we
cooked them", "they were tasty". Then to continue the story, link whatever comes next with "seeing
that they were tasty..." This theme-distribute-link story structure strikes some as uniquely Papuan
or nearly so. Speaking of yams three, we may all be doing
math "in a decimal world" these days, but Niugini is home to the less common in our modern world
base 20 and to the bulk of the world's languages with rarer bases. Base 2 anyone? How about
15? Add to this the unrivaled art of body part tallies to count symmetrically up to
a base, 27 in some languages, or cycle to higher numbers. So from numerals to narration to negation
to no passives to possession, object-verb order with postpositions, sounds like ngma
and mba and small inventories, have we found the distinctive telltale Papuan characteristics?
Well, bring back our simple starting tale behind the term. Papuan means all the scores of non-Australian
and especially non-Austronesian families and isolates inside this ellipse. Papuan speakers
were hunter-gatherers here long before technology-bearing seafarers sailed ashore, then returned to
the waves as they carried Oceanic tongues ever deeper into the Pacific. We've already pulled on threads that begin
to unravel this tidy tale. How many of those Papuan ingredients are also cooked into the
Oceanic branch of Austronesian since its earliest days? What about the west edge of the Papuasphere,
where Austronesian languages like Alorese have such heavy grammatical influence in their
ancestry, and in Taba the high speech level is replete with West Papuan words? What about
the east edge, where the backstory to Vanuatu involves Papuans and Austronesians arriving
together on the same canoes? And what of the middle, where the Torres Strait is home to
both a Papuan and an Australian language? The residual, negative, "non-"them use of
"Papuan" covers up the true messiness of belonging to a dynamic language area and developing
from a network of families. Hence one author challenges us that to be consistent, Papuan
must include more than exclude. Yet again we are right back to the people. Rather
than passively awaiting Austronesian expansion, these features are evidence that Papuans crossed
seas, developed cultures and spoke languages from many families, including eventually Oceanic.
Influence from their food technology even extends into our lives - my last video was
about how we outsiders only recently began to appreciate this cradle of agriculture and
languages. Here we are again, starting with languages
and ending with a deeper point. So let me reshare links to local voices and ways to
support Indigenous efforts in the area and beyond, plus my sources. Thanks to patrons
for your patronage. Hope you enjoyed one with more grammar; no guarantees I won't go back

took this word on and replaced their own, their taro or talo, with ma. Is this proof of a language-agricultural spread,
a "langriculture"? It's interesting but maybe less than compelling. "Ma" is so widely borrowed, and exactly who
first domesticated taro and where is a surprisingly difficult question to answer. Want to know what's more compelling? Invite them back in, say their names again:
sugarcane and bananas. Compared to their tuberous friends, these
crops have stable names across the family. Just for fun, one linguist asks us to consider
the possibility that this word for banana even journeyed with the fruit to India, then
all the way into Arabic and Latin. Sugarcane and banana are not just snacks here. They are staples. Yes, even sugarcane, unlike elsewhere in the
world, is a dietary and social centerpiece. Connecting the words to the plants to the
culture, we arrive at the hypothesis of a whole linguistic-agricultural package fueling
the TNG family. A shared, intertwined langriculture. Not sure how much it's right but it's not
looking wrong. While researchers at the Kuk Swamp site were
turning the tables and proving that New Guinea had early independent agricultural origins,
the local Kawelka people were on the move. In an "efficiently executed operation", they
reclaimed their traditional land and built homes and gardens to inaugurate a new, living
agricultural phase. This act culminated in nomination as a world
heritage site and their recognition as the "ground root" people of the land. If you're watching after this many minutes
you have at least some care for somewhere that is far from your home. Unless you're one of my awesome viewers from
the island, I see you! I hope you'll stay for one more big connection. Like all good stories about language and food,
this one is ultimately about people. Because when their longstanding commitment
to a langricultural package collides with the present, it leads them to ask a very Papuan
question. First context, so this question lands for
you like it did for me. Imagination vs reality. Imagination first: look up the Indigenous
peoples of New Guinea. (Just search "Papuan people" on this platform.) Results are almost mythic: somehow preserved, pristine, isolated. That's how educated outsiders even see them, as
ripe for research, conversion, building highways, extracting resources, running news stories
about every new "uncontacted" people in an "untouched" rainforest. Here's the reality behind that imagination:
the villages, the managed food forests the languages aren't untouched at all. Even the very act of studying languages on
this big big island is so ultimately connected to colonization, and turning land and food into
cash rocks and cash crops. Which brings us to the next and final moment
we'll spend together before we part. Like you heard before, but once more. In the Highlands of New Guinea, traditionally
people welcome guests with a big sugarcane. For many sugarcane is a huge part of the diet. Others are sustained mostly by bananas. See, here these things are not treats or snacks,
but key pieces of life with deep linguistic roots and a very long agricultural backstory. But that's not the future everyone wants. So, what grows but does not change? That's been a guiding question for me as I researched,
wrote, dreamt of and narrated this video. One possible answer has been here the whole
time... and it's bananas! Vegetative propagation makes for great growth
but makes change difficult. It entails that as plants produce they stay
consistent. But ask it another way, the way I've been learning
to: what is the good life? TNG expansion has been able to include new plants and
new people while remaining rooted in vegeculture. But today it faces serious linguistic competition
from English-derived creole Tok Pisin, and agriculturally from exported seedy crops:

this ↻ ...or this ↗?  You may not know Papuan people, but they know
you. What you have, your stuff. On this island, "nearly a continent" of biodiversity

people will not make food gardens. Money will buy a house, money will buy food. People will depend on money." And maybe then "there will be no more gardens,
because the population will increase... School will be to them what gardens are to
us today." Or instead what if, "We will no longer destroy
the trees. The conservation area will expand. All trees will grow again. The forest will thrive." I haven't yet got enough dirt on my fingers
to answer wisely, but one anonymous farmer from Ohu in the north offers a choice: "We

which one do you want to swallow?" Thanks for watching. On many channels here

## 25. Why West Africa keeps inventing writing systems
**Channel:** NativLang | **Views:** 913K | **Date:** 4 years ago | **Duration:** 10:07 | **ID:** xa8BYZrSTxY
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=xa8BYZrSTxY

### Transcript:
West Africa has been busy lately. Busy inventing
writing. Since the 1800s, this area has become home to dozens of brand new scripts. One paper
I'm reading counts at least twenty-six. Can most of us even name that many scripts from
outside Africa!? What is happening here? Why so many? Today let's get curious about West
Africa and this new chapter they're adding to the history of writing. The south of Guinea. Two young brothers have
locked themselves in their room to complete an unusual task: closing their eyes and drawing
lines. At the ages of ten and fourteen, together they're deciding on shapes of signs, envisioning
how a people's writing system will work at the very start of an entire script's history. This history isn't ancient. It's not that
old at all. The brothers are Abdoulaye and Ibrahima, and the year is 1986. Back then,
if you were from among their Fula people and wanted to pass a message to a relative, you'd
get a letter transcribed in Ajami, basically Arabic adapted to kind of fit your language.
You'd bring it to a taxi or anyone heading towards the destination, and hopefully find
someone skilled in guessing your native words that the Ajami script represented. Someone
like Ibrahim's and Abdoulaye's dad, who is very good at this Ajami letter guessing game. The
brothers follow in his footsteps, get good at it, too. But they want more. They want
a script meant for their language and their people. They invent a script that has come to be known
as Adlam. It's spreading quickly throughout their community of traditionally nomadic people
living across many countries, including among women who didn't attend school in English
or French. Today, Fula people teach classes in Adlam, write letters and books in Adlam
and post and text in Adlam on their phones. The brothers would hardly be the first or
the last to create a brand new script for one of West Africa's tongues. Decades before Adlam, in the 1940s, prolific
author and language scholar Sulemana Kantɛ is vexed by how a Lebanese journalist dismisses
his native language: unlike colonial literate cultures, Africa's Indigenous voices are as
impossible to capture in writing as birds chirping. Instead of taking this as a decisive
putdown, these words strike Kantɛ as a challenge. He unveils a new standard for Manding languages,
embracing many dialects at once by naming the script with a word they all share: N'ko, "I say".
N'ko goes on to become one of Africa's most successful scripts. Adlam and N'ko both have characteristics that
may remind you of Arabic: written right to left, often or always in a cursive connected
style. They also have things reminiscent of Latin script: full vowel letters. And numbers
that run in the same direction as the text. (Arabic!!) But the symbols, and the features,
are their own. Of course, we could walk down this list of
well over twenty other West African scripts, with a story behind each one: Ndebe, Garay,
Bété, Yoruba Holy Writing, Shü-mom, and back two centuries to the 1800s and the Vai
syllabary. In a broader African context, this list of
scripts is not alone either. Over the same time period, far to the east Beria have adopted and
popularized camel brand writing. Old southern African symbology gets crafted into a new Bantu
writing system. And there are multiple home-grown Somali scripts. But the sheer number makes
West Africa a writing systems hotbed. Why invent so many scripts though? Where does this
come from? At face value, these scripts solve a technical
linguistic problem: Ajami doesn't represent Maninka or Fula well, or Latin doesn't represent
Igbo or Vai. Consider this need, this challenge in its place,
in the context of where we are. West Africa is an area full of unique features you'd have
to account for when creating a writing system, including features that are rare elsewhere. Here are two different Fula words. Yes, different!
In the first word, the nasal n and the d fall in two separate syllables: hin-du. Ok. But
the second requires a bit of explanation. So, West Africa has prenasalized consonants.
You start a stop sound in your nose — m, n, ŋ — and then immediately raise your
soft palate to release it through your mouth – b, d, g – all in a single unit: mbe, ngo,
and... ndu. That's why the second word is a different word, also two syllables. It's
hin-du vs hi-ndu. Well, Adlam has a symbol for that! Two more words for you. What's the difference here?
Like many languages you've met before if you've been listening to my stories over the years,
Fula varieties distinguish short and long vowels. So there's a continuum of Fula languages.
One of them is known as Pular with a short a, and another one is called Pulaar with long
a. Adlam has a symbol to represent that, too! What just may be the rarest of West Africa's
sounds are these kind of two sounds in one. Brace your articulators! Put your tongue in
position to say a g and your lips in position to say a b. Try to release both of them at
the same time: ɡ͡b. And without voicing: k͡p. Or nasal: ŋ͡m. So instead of calling
this language Ig-bo, try saying I-ɡ͡bo. (And here's an extra credit challenge from Vai: what
happens if you prenasalize this sound?) Wow, you're getting a phonetic workout today. These
sounds are so common in the area's names and words that Adlam has symbols dedicated to
them. A triangle pointing downwards is the N'ko
letter for this double sound. But N'ko also supports some West African features missing
from Adlam. So N'ko has seven vowel letters for the seven vowels of Manding languages.
And, as usual in this part of Africa, Manding vowels are tonal. In fact, south of the Sahara,
most languages care about tone. Wolof and Fula are exceptional for not doing this. And
that's why Kantɛ gave N'ko seven tone marks, combining short and long pronunciation of
vowels with tones that are falling or rising or low or high. (...Hmm.) One last West African feature: nasal vowels.
This area is very into nasalization, with languages that distinguish regular versus
nasal vowels in short and long flavors, or even spreading nasalization across nearby
vowels in a way that'd make you think you were hearing Guaraní. And N'ko handles this.
It equips nasal vowels with a simple dot below. So all combined, N'ko has a compact way to represent
nasalization, length and tone for the vowels of Manding languages. And the Vai script has symbols for all of
these except tone. Just as rows of Vai signs represent what you might consider "normal"
consonant-vowel syllables, so too are there rows of signs for prenasalized and for doubly
articulated sounds. Not specially marked. Not treated as unique cases. Each is just
another sign fit for a language where they are natively effortless, everyday sounds. That's not the whole story though. Each of
these scripts isn't just a linguistic exercise. That's actually not the main reason why they
exist. There's more context here. The context of a less-told history where West Africa has
long used visual graphic codes. Ancient Nsibidi symbols, still the inspiration behind multiple
of these modern scripts. The Adinkra signs in Ghana and Côte d'Ivoire. Or the countless famed
multilingual medieval manuscripts of Timbuktu. All of this before colonization. Rupture. These many scripts are about people finding
an Indigenous West African vision of the written word. Often a literal vision. We're told the
inspiration for the Vai script and for Mandombe came in a dream. For N'ko, it was almost a
dare to show off that West African languages are literary and writable. And ADLaM, its
very name has become an acrophonic definition for the script's grassroots community: the
letters that protect the people from vanishing. At the start, I recounted how two young brothers
stood at the root of an entire script's history. Well, most of that history looks yet to be
written. Thank you for watching. And to my patrons
for supporting me and being so encouraging while I learned and animated. Ah, this the
part where I say: stick around and subscribe for language.

a Dutch variety becoming Afrikaans. There are also pidgins and creoles. 
Some people feel like the two examples   you're looking at here are bad 
Arabic or broken Portuguese,   but no, linguistically they are creoles 
lexified by those languages.
   Colonization also expands our 
understanding outward from Africa.   Colonizers forced African people, tongues, hands 
and the voices and signs they carried into the   waters across the globe, making a massive impact 
on language history. Their descendants in the   diaspora today speak former colonial languages, 
like English. Indigenous languages, like Garifuna.   And creole languages like Kreyòl 
Ayisyen or Papiamentu. And in all cases,   Africa's languages from those not-so-five families 
leave imprints on words, meaning and grammar. In the end, yeah, there may 
be no one African language,   single quintessential family, place or people. 
Maybe with this time we've spent together we can   appreciate the many languages of Africa, 
and the peoples who speak them, better. And if you want more, last year I put 
together this list full of creators   and channels who share more about this 
continent with the longest linguistic   history on earth. Please learn from them. 
And stick around and subscribe for language.

of phonetic syllable glyphs, and it reappears here in the first glyph block, just to give
you a taste of the versatility. And to read the rest, you follow the traditional path: zig-zag between two columns before moving over to the next two columns. But it might be a while before we're searching
and posting on the web using these glyphs. Mayan languages mostly get written in Latin
characters, as they have been for nearly five centuries. There is hope and research going into figuring
out how Maya glyphs could be encoded in Unicode. The way the script works, it's a surprisingly
big challenge. I mean, this is the first script to make me
add a third dimension to my reading axes. As much as I find glyphic history fascinating
(oh it is), I'm more intrigued by where the scripts, and people who speak the languages
today, will go from here. Developing your own quick handwriting? Inventing new glyphs? Reawakening more of the scripts? Since the word for write also means paint,

This block with its 62 symbols may change

because, well, "we need to do our jobs". The quarry, though, doubles as an archaeological

a call: "Hello, archaeology!?" Two archaeologists roll into town, take photos
and begin identification. The initial results are stunning. A few of the finds date from the same time

Preclassic era, including the star of our story – this block, 36 by 21 by 13 centimeters,
full of what look like glyphs written roughly in rows. Experts in Mesoamerican epigraphy take note

if the Americas even had "real" writing at all. Not always as a criticism. Maybe these are rich works of meaningful iconography. Maybe "high culture" doesn't require writing at all. We've since learned otherwise. (Not about culture. You can absolutely go cultural without going
scriptural!) No, the world has conclusively learned that
Mesoamerica birthed writing. Ancient writing, and lots of it. Libraries across Europe, from Oxford to Bologna,

āmoxtli aren't bound books, they're one long folded and painted hide or bark āmatl paper,

measures. We absolutely should get into decipherment here,
the arguments and uncertainties, but how we're now learning to read Nāhua glyphs is also
for another time. All told, it implies this civilization started
writing more than 500 years ago. Some of the oldest painted books held in European
stashes aren't Aztec, Mexica, but from peoples of Oaxaca that the Mexica called Mixtec, people
of the cloud, adapted from their names: Rain People. Rain glyph painters are known for their sophisticated
books full of ancestral history, and these may push our date back to 700 years into the
past. One epic city in the Valley of Mexico was
already using glyphs much earlier. Teotihuacán, long celebrated for its huge
monuments, turned up unexpected finds. Not on its giant pyramids but down in the
homes within what's come to be called "la plaza de los glifos". Now the language of the Teotihuacanos is notoriously
up for debate, but at over 1500 years old, their glyphs more than double our time horizon. (I know, I know, you're older. We'll get back to you, promise!) Out east, a famously literate civilization
uses writing seemingly everywhere: folded books, outdoor monuments, indoor monuments,

hold their breath for scientific breakthroughs before they can even be opened without disintegrating. (And if it looks like art not writing, yes and...
you can be both! And when the time comes to animate what Classic
Maya sounded like, know that I'm preparing for it.) But older Maya glyphs show up in stucco or

and Tak'alik Abaj. These cities were not Maya... not exclusively. At a crossroads of Mesoamerica's many languages
from many families, these sites may get overlooked now, but millennia ago they predated and surpassed
their neighbors in structure building... and glyph writing. The language could be Mixe-Zoque? Maya? Both? But it's no stretch to say they were writing
2000 years ago. But that's still late. Update! A brief report on finds from Guatemala swoops
in and suddenly pushes back the date for the earliest Maya writing to at least 2200 or
2300 years ago. Amidst the frenzy of interest around Maya,
inscriptions come to light from what's long suspected of being Mesoamerica's "mother",
but is honestly more like your precocious cousin. Olmec sites turn up amazing structures...
and early dates. Standouts include representations of great
figures and the famously massive heads. But writing here looks somewhat younger, in
a script called "Isthmian". Examples are few but exciting. A stela pulled from a river near La Mojarra
is the longest. Despite claims that this is definitely Zoque
and definitely decipherable, the jury's out. The oldest though come from Chiapas: the earliest long count date yet found anywhere, and a piece of pottery with signs
2400 years old. The sun rises ever earlier on Mesoamerican
writing. Meanwhile, elsewhere, there's a place of clouds
we need to visit before our story circles right back to its start. An unexpectedly fertile land for scribes lies
west. It seems an unlikely contender from where
we've spent time, but seeing the impressive sites rise above the Valleys of Oaxaca, you
might be amazed but not surprised to learn that Oto-Manguean peoples here have native
